Non-Homotopic Drawings of Multigraphs
Authors: António Girão, Freddie Illingworth, Alex Scott, David R. Wood
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
A multigraph drawn in the plane is non-homotopic if no two edges connecting the same pair of vertices can be continuously deformed into each other without passing through a vertex, and is k-crossing if every pair of edges (self-)intersects at most k times. We prove that the number of edges in an n-vertex non-homotopic k-crossing multigraph is at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$6^{13 n (k + 1)}$$\end{document}613n(k+1), which is a big improvement over previous upper bounds. We also study this problem in the setting of monotone drawings where every edge is an x-monotone curve. We show that the number of edges, m, in such a drawing is at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ k + 1\end{array}}\right) $$\end{document}22nk+1 and the number of crossings is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \bigl (\frac{m^{2 + 1/k}}{n^{1 + 1/k}}\bigr )$$\end{document}Ω(m2+1/kn1+1/k). For fixed k these bounds are both best possible up to a constant multiplicative factor.

Introduction

A central question of graph drawing research asks for the maximum number of edges in an n-vertex graph that admits a drawing satisfying certain properties. For example, it follows from Euler’s formula that planar graphs have at most 3n-63n-6 edges (for n ⩾ 3n⩾3), which is tight for planar triangulations. The situation for multigraphs (allowing parallel edges and self-loops) is less clear, since there are planar multigraphs with one vertex and arbitrarily many edges. To avoid this, the constraint that the drawing is non-homotopic is typically applied: a drawing is non-homotopic if no pair of parallel edges or loops can be continuously deformed into each other without passing through vertices. The planar condition is also frequently relaxed so that the drawing is k-crossing: the number of crossings between each pair of edges is at most k and the number of self-intersections of each edge is at most k. Note that some bound on the number of crossings is required as there are non-homotopic drawings on a fixed number of vertices with arbitrarily many edges where different edges wind round the same vertex increasingly many times, as illustrated in Figure 1.Fig. 1Non-homotopic edges (with no self-crossings) winding around the same vertex increasingly many times

Non-homotopic edges (with no self-crossings) winding around the same vertex increasingly many times

In 1996, Juvan, Malnič and Mohar [12, Thm. 3.5] proved that n-vertex multigraphs with a non-homotopic k-crossing drawing have a bounded number of edges. Following this, the maximum cardinality of a collection of arcs that is non-homotopic and k-crossing on various surfaces has been well-studied. See, for example, the papers [2–5, 7, 10, 15, 17, 21, 22].

The first result of this paper is an improved upper bound on the number of edges in a non-homotopic k-crossing drawing of a multigraph.

Theorem 1.1

Let n, k be positive integers. Let G be an n-vertex multigraph that has a non-homotopic k-crossing drawing. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |E(G)| \leqslant 6^{13 n (k + 1)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|E(G)|⩽613n(k+1).

Theorem 1.1 improves on results from the literature, as we now explain. For integers n, k ⩾ 1n,k⩾1, let S denote the set obtained from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 by removing n distinct points, and fix x∈ Sx∈S. Let f(n, k) be the maximum number of pairwise non-homotopic x-loops in S such that none of them passes through x, each of them has fewer than k self-intersections, and every pair of them cross fewer than k times. The argument of Juvan, Malnič and Mohar [12] shows that1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(n, k) < (nk)^{\mathcal {O}(nk^2)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(n,k)<(nk)O(nk2).Theorem 1.1 (in the special case that every edge is a loop incident to a single vertex) improves the upper bound on f(n, k) to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(n, k) \leqslant 6^{13 n k}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(n,k)⩽613nk.Note that Pach, Tardos and Tóth [17] proved the exponential lower bound,2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(n, k) \geqslant 2^{\sqrt{nk}/3}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(n,k)⩾2nk/3.for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \geqslant n \geqslant 2$$\end{document}k⩾n⩾2.

Another common constraint applied to graph drawings is monotonicity: a drawing is monotone if every edge is an x-monotone curve. See [6, 8, 16, 18, 19] for previous work on crossings in monotone drawings. The second direction of this paper is to obtain a bound on the number of edges in a multigraph with a monotone non-homotopic k-crossing drawing, and to prove an associated crossing lemma. We also derive the corresponding results when every pair of parallel edges cross at most k times and every pair of incident edges cross at most k times (a pair of edges is incident if they share at least one common end-vertex). All of these results have optimal dependence on n.

Let k < nk<n be non-negative integers. If each pair of parallel edges cross at most k times in D, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|E(G)| \leqslant n(n - 1) \cdot \left( {\begin{array}{c}2 n\\ k + 1\end{array}}\right) $$\end{document}|E(G)|⩽n(n-1)·2nk+1.

The condition k < nk<n is fully general: if an n-vertex multigraph G has a monotone drawing, then it has a homotopically equivalent monotone drawing in which every pair of edges cross fewer than n times.1 In Section 3 we give examples that show the dependence upon n is best possible in all three parts.

One motivation for such extremal theorems is that they are essential ingredients in proofs of crossing number lower bounds. The famous ‘Crossing Lemma’ of Ajtai, Chvátal, Newborn and Szemerédi [1] and Leighton [14] says that every drawing of any n-vertex simple graph (with no parallel edges and no loops) with m ⩾ 4nm⩾4n edges has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \bigl (\frac{m^3}{n^2}\bigr )$$\end{document}Ω(m3n2) crossings. Székely [23] proved an analogous result for multigraphs where the lower bound depends on the edge multiplicity. Recent research has shown that under certain assumptions, no dependence on the multiplicity is needed [13, 17, 20]. Similarly, using Theorem 1.2, we prove the following three tight lower bounds on the number of crossings in monotone non-homotopic drawings where each pair of parallel edges/incident edges/edges cross at most k times.

We now compare Thoerems 1.2 and 1.3 to related results in the literature. Here we conflate a vertex or edge with its image in a drawing. Pach and Tóth [20] and Kaufmann, Pach, Tóth, and Ueckerdt [13] defined a drawing of a multigraph to be:separated if any two parallel edges do not cross and the “lens” formed by their union has at least one vertex in its interior and at least one vertex in its exterior;single-crossing if any pair of edges cross at most once;locally star-like if no two edges with a common end-vertex cross.Note that every separated drawing is non-homotopic but there are non-homotopic drawings that are not separated, since parallel edges might cross in a non-homotopic drawing. Also note that locally star-like is the same as each pair of incident edges cross at most 0 times.

separated if any two parallel edges do not cross and the “lens” formed by their union has at least one vertex in its interior and at least one vertex in its exterior;

separated if any two parallel edges do not cross and the “lens” formed by their union has at least one vertex in its interior and at least one vertex in its exterior;

single-crossing if any pair of edges cross at most once;

single-crossing if any pair of edges cross at most once;

locally star-like if no two edges with a common end-vertex cross.

locally star-like if no two edges with a common end-vertex cross.

Pach and Tóth [20] defined a drawing to be branching if it is separated, single-crossing, and locally star-like. They proved that the number of edges, m, in a branching drawing on n vertices is at most n(n - 2)n(n-2) and the number of crossings is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \bigl (\frac{m^3}{n^2}\bigr )$$\end{document}Ω(m3n2). Kaufmann, Pach, Tóth, and Ueckerdt [13] proved that these results still hold without the single-crossing assumption. Theorem 1.2 and Theorem 1.3(b) with k = 0k=0 match these bounds (up to multiplicative constants), replacing the separated assumption by the weaker non-homotopic assumption and adding the monotonicity assumption. Kaufmann, Pach, Tóth, and Ueckerdt [13] conjectured that every separated single-crossing (but not necessarily locally star-like) drawing on n vertices has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(n^2)$$\end{document}O(n2) edges. Fox, Pach and Suk [7] proved a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(n^2\log n)$$\end{document}O(n2logn) upper bound in this case. Theorem 1.2(a) in the k = 1k=1 case gives a better bound on the number of edges without the assumption that parallel edges do not cross, and with the extra monotonicity assumption.

The rest of the paper is structured as follows. We introduce formal notation in Section 1.1. In Section 2 we prove Theorem 1.1. The examples showing the tightness of Theorems 1.2 and 1.3 are given in Section 3. We prove Theorem 1.2 in Section 4 and Theorem 1.3 in Section 5. We finish with open problems in Section 6.

We finish this introduction with a brief outline of the combinatorial idea appearing in the proof of Theorem 1.1 (the proof of Theorem 1.2 is a much simpler version of this). Suppose G is an n-vertex multigraph drawn in the plane. We will define a ‘frame’ for G. This will be a small set of arcs (that are sub-curves of edges of G) and points in the plane such that if two edges of G ‘interact’ with the frame in the same way, then those edges are homotopic. We will record how an edge interacts with the frame using a signature: a list of the arcs of the frame that the edge crosses as well as some winding numbers around some points of the frame. We will record the signature in such a way that if two edges have the same signature, then they are homotopic. Thus, if the drawing is non-homotopic, then the number of edges is at most the number of possible signatures. Finally we will show that if the drawing is k-crossing, then the number of possible signatures may be bounded from above by a function of n and k.

Note added in revision. The referee of this paper pointed out the important work of Przytycki [21, Thm. 1.2 & 1.5], which predates references [7, 13, 20], and implies the above-mentioned conjecture in [13] as well as Theorem 1.2 (with slightly less precise bounds). In fact, the result of Przytycki [21] is significantly more general than Theorem 1.2 (replacing the monotone condition by a weaker assumption, and it works on any surface). The topological proof of Przytycki [21] is completely different to our combinatorial proof. Also, our construction of monotone drawings in Section 3 has a similar flavour to (but more edges than) a construction of Przytycki [21, Example 4.1].

Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2 \setminus \{\phi (x) :x\in V(G)\setminus \{v,w\}\}$$\end{document}R2\{ϕ(x):x∈V(G)\{v,w}} is the plane with |V(G)| - 2|V(G)|-2 puncture points. Intuitively, parallel edges e and f are homotopic if e can be continuously deformed into f without passing through any vertices and while keeping the end-points fixed. As illustrated in Figure 2, a drawing of a multigraph is non-homotopic if no two parallel edges are homotopic. It is natural to consider non-homotopic drawings, since homotopic edges can be considered to be equivalent. The non-homotopic assumption immediately eliminates the example of a planar multigraph with exactly one vertex and more than one edge.Fig. 2Non-homotopic parallel edges

Non-homotopic parallel edges

A drawing ϕ ϕ of a multigraph G is monotone if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _e$$\end{document}ϕe is an x-monotone curve for each edge e∈ E(G)e∈E(G); that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _e$$\end{document}ϕe intersects each vertical line in at most one point. Note that a monotone drawing has no self-loops and no edge self-intersects.

Non-Homotopic Drawings

In this section we prove Theorem 1.1, which says that every non-homotopic k-crossing drawing on n vertices has at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$6^{13n(k+1)}$$\end{document}613n(k+1) edges. This result follows quickly from the next lemma.

Lemma 2.1

Let n, k be positive integers. Let x, y be two (not necessarily distinct) vertices of an n-vertex multigraph G such that every edge of G is from x to y and G has a non-homotopic k-crossing drawing. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |E(G)| \leqslant 6^{13 n (k + 1)}/n^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|E(G)|⩽613n(k+1)/n2.

Proof of Theorem 1.1

Let G be an n-vertex multigraph as in the statement of Theorem 1.1. For x, y ∈ V(G)x,y∈V(G), let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{x, y}$$\end{document}Gx,y be the subgraph of G consisting of the edges from x to y. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{x, y}$$\end{document}Gx,y satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma 2.1, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|E(G_{x, y})| \leqslant 6^{13 n (k + 1)}/n^2$$\end{document}|E(Gx,y)|⩽613n(k+1)/n2. The result follows since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E(G) = \bigcup _{x, y} E(G_{x, y})$$\end{document}E(G)=⋃x,yE(Gx,y). □ □

In fact, Lemma 2.1 implies that Theorem 1.1 holds even with the k-crossing assumption weakened to every pair of parallel edges cross at most k times, every pair of loops incident to the same vertex cross at most k times, and every edge has at most k self-intersections.

Before proving Lemma 2.1 we need a simple lemma about plane graphs as well as some basic facts about the winding number. We will think of each edge e of a plane graph as having two sides, labelled \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^+$$\end{document}e+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^-$$\end{document}e- arbitrarily. If e is incident to two faces, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^+$$\end{document}e+ is incident to one of these faces and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^-$$\end{document}e- to the other. If e is incident to only one face, then both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^+$$\end{document}e+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^-$$\end{document}e- are both incident to this face. See Figure 3.Fig. 3(a) Side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^+$$\end{document}e+ is incident to face \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1$$\end{document}C1, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^-$$\end{document}e- is incident to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_2$$\end{document}C2. Lemma 2.2

Let G be a plane graph in which no vertices are isolated, all but at most r vertices have degree at least three, and every face is incident to at least seven sides. Then |V(G)| ⩽ 10r - 28|V(G)|⩽10r-28.

Proof

Let n, m, F denote the number of vertices, edges and faces of G, respectively. Consider all pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(e^{\bullet }, f)$$\end{document}(e∙,f) where f is a face of G and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^\bullet $$\end{document}e∙ is a side of an edge of G that is incident to f. Every edge e corresponds to two such pairs (one for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^+$$\end{document}e+ and one for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^-$$\end{document}e-) and every face is in at least seven, so 7F ⩽ 2m7F⩽2m. Hence, by Euler’s formula,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} n + \tfrac{2}{7} m \geqslant n + F \geqslant m + 2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}n+27m⩾n+F⩾m+2.Thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m \leqslant \frac{7}{5} (n - 2)$$\end{document}m⩽75(n-2). Finally, since G has minimum degree at least one,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tfrac{14}{5} (n - 2) \geqslant 2m = \sum _{v \in V(G)} \deg (v) \geqslant 3(n - r) + r = 3n - 2r. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}145(n-2)⩾2m=∑v∈V(G)deg(v)⩾3(n-r)+r=3n-2r.Rearranging gives the required result. □ □

The following facts about the winding number can be found in most texts on complex analysis or algebraic topology (such as [9]). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma :[0, 1] \rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}γ:[0,1]→C be a continuous closed curve (so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma (0) = \gamma (1)$$\end{document}γ(0)=γ(1)). Lemma 2.3

Proof

We first address part 1. Viewing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0$$\end{document}γ0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _1$$\end{document}γ1 as continuous functions from [0, 1] to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}C, there are continuous functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_0, r_1 :[0, 1] \rightarrow \mathbb {R}_{>0}$$\end{document}r0,r1:[0,1]→R>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _0, \theta _1 :[0, 1] \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}θ0,θ1:[0,1]→R such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _j = a + r_j e^{2 \pi i \theta _j}$$\end{document}γj=a+rje2πiθj. First suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _0$$\end{document}γ0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _1$$\end{document}γ1 have the same winding number I, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I = \theta _1(1) - \theta _1(0) = \theta _0(1) - \theta _0(0)$$\end{document}I=θ1(1)-θ1(0)=θ0(1)-θ0(0). Also, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (1, t) = \theta (0, t) + I$$\end{document}θ(1,t)=θ(0,t)+I and so H(· , t)H(·,t) is a closed curve for each t. We now address part 2. We will prove the following equivalent statement by induction on ℓ ℓ: if γ γ self-intersects ℓ ℓ times, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|I(\gamma , a)| \leqslant \ell + 1$$\end{document}|I(γ,a)|⩽ℓ+1. First suppose that ℓ = 0ℓ=0. Then γ γ is a simple closed curve. By the Jordan Curve Theorem, γ γ splits the plane into two regions: an interior region bounded by γ γ and an exterior unbounded region. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I(\gamma , \cdot )$$\end{document}I(γ,·) is zero on the unbounded region and is ± 1±1 on the interior region (since the interior and exterior regions are adjacent). Now suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \geqslant 1$$\end{document}ℓ⩾1. Let b be a point of self-intersection of γ γ. We may partition γ γ into two closed curves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{(0)}$$\end{document}γ(0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{(1)}$$\end{document}γ(1) that meet at b. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{(j)}$$\end{document}γ(j) have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _j$$\end{document}ℓj self-intersection. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \geqslant \ell _0 + \ell _1 + 1$$\end{document}ℓ⩾ℓ0+ℓ1+1 (the +1+1 is for the self-intersection at b). Finally, by induction on ℓ ℓ,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |I(\gamma , a)|&= |I(\gamma ^{(0)}, a) + I(\gamma ^{(1)}, a)| \leqslant |I(\gamma ^{(0)}, a)|\\&\quad + |I(\gamma ^{(1)}, a)| \leqslant (\ell _0 + 1) + (\ell _1 + 1) \leqslant \ell + 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|I(γ,a)|=|I(γ(0),a)+I(γ(1),a)|⩽|I(γ(0),a)|+|I(γ(1),a)|⩽(ℓ0+1)+(ℓ1+1)⩽ℓ+1.□ □

We are finally ready to prove Lemma 2.1.

Proof of Lemma 1.1

We introduce some notation. Suppose that H is a graph drawn in the plane and E' ⊆ E(H)E′⊆E(H) is a set of edges. We write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}(E')$$\end{document}C(E′) for the union of the arcs given by the edges of E'E′. This includes all end-points of edges of E'E′. For example, if H is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_3$$\end{document}K3 with vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{(0,0), (1,0), (0,1)\}$$\end{document}{(0,0),(1,0),(0,1)} and straight edges, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}(E(H))$$\end{document}C(E(H)) is the perimeter of the right-angled triangle with vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{(0,0), (1,0), (0,1)\}$$\end{document}{(0,0),(1,0),(0,1)}.

We will first prove the result under the assumption that x ≠ yx≠y and at the end of the proof we will explain how the strategy is adapted to handle the x = yx=y case. Let the vertices of G be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x, y, v_1, \dotsc , v_{n - 2}$$\end{document}x,y,v1,⋯,vn-2.

Fix a non-homotopic drawing of G in which every pair of edges cross at most k times. Recall that all edges of G are from x to y. By applying a small perturbation we may and will assume that no three edges meet at a point. For i = 1, …c , n - 2i=1,⋯,n-2, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_i$$\end{document}ei be an edge of G passing closest to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_i$$\end{document}vi (the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_i$$\end{document}ei may not be distinct). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_i$$\end{document}pi be a point on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_i$$\end{document}ei closest to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_i$$\end{document}vi (the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_i$$\end{document}pi may not all be distinct, some of them may be crossings and some of them may be x or y). By the minimality of the length of line-segment \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_i v_i$$\end{document}pivi, no edge of G crosses the interior of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_i v_i$$\end{document}pivi.

We are going to build a ‘frame’ F which we will use to encode the homotopy of edges of G: edges of G that intersect F ‘in the same way’ will be homotopic. The frame F will be a connected plane graph with V(G) ⊆ V(F)V(G)⊆V(F). We first build a frame \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_n$$\end{document}Fn that will be a plane tree containing all vertices of G. We use f to refer to edges of a frame and e for edges of G.

The process stops when, for every side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^\circ $$\end{document}f∘ (f ∈ E(F)f∈E(F), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\circ \in \{+, -\}$$\end{document}∘∈{+,-}) and every face C of F to which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^\circ $$\end{document}f∘ is incident, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^\circ $$\end{document}f∘ sees at most eight other sides across C. We now show the number of steps in this process is at most 5(n - 1)5(n-1) and the final frame has at most 10n edges. First note that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_n$$\end{document}Fn has at most four edges, then no steps will be carried out and we have the required bounds. Otherwise, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_n$$\end{document}Fn is a plane tree with at least five edges in which at most n vertices have degree less than three. Every step introduces two vertices of degree three and splits a face into two faces both of which are incident to at least seven sides. Hence, every frame in the process is a connected plane graph, has at most n vertices of degree less than 3 and every face of F is incident to at least seven sides. Thus, by Lemma 2.2, every frame within the process has at most 10n - 28 ⩽ 10n10n-28⩽10n vertices. Since each step adds two vertices, the process must stop within 5n - 14 ⩽ 5(n - 1)5n-14⩽5(n-1) steps.

Signature

By Lemma 2.3, for a point p in the plane, two closed curves in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2 - p$$\end{document}R2-p are homotopic if and only if they have the same winding number around p. Since the closed curve created here never enters an inner face of F, the winding number I(e')I(e′) determines e'e′ up to homotopy. Similarly, if an edge e goes from x to an outer edge f across \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{\infty }$$\end{document}C∞ (call this portion e'e′) we form a closed curve consisting of e'e′, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{x, f}$$\end{document}Px,f as well as the part of f that joins these up and define I(e')I(e′) to be the winding number of this closed curve about \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{x, f}$$\end{document}px,f. Define I(e')I(e′) similarly if an edge e goes from an outer edge f to y.

Theorem 1.1 applies for k ⩾ 1k⩾1. The k = 0k=0 case (with no crossings) is handled by the following elementary result.

Proposition 2.4

Every non-homotopic plane multigraph on n ⩾ 2n⩾2 vertices has at most 4n - 44n-4 edges.

Proof

Let G be a non-homotopic plane multigraph on n ⩾ 2n⩾2 vertices with m edges. By adding edges, we may assume that G is connected.

A loop of G is trivial if it has no vertices in its interior. Any two trivial loops incident to the same vertex are homotopic, so G has at most n trivial loops. Let G'G′ be obtained from G by deleting all the trivial loops, and let m' :=|E(G')|m′:=|E(G′)|. Then m ⩽ m' + nm⩽m′+n. We claim that m' ⩽ 3n - 4m′⩽3n-4 which will suffice. Since G is connected, so too is G'G′. Define the length of a face F to be the number of edges (counting multiplicity) in a walk around F. As an example, the length of the face F shown in Figure 7 is 3 (the edge e is traversed twice, once along each side, in a walk around F).

Consider a face F of length at most 2. If F has a vertex in its interior, then, since G'G′ is connected, there is an edge from an interior vertex to a vertex on the boundary of F and so F has length at least 3. Thus F has no vertex in its interior. The boundary of F must either be a loop or a pair of parallel edges between two vertices u and v. Since G'G′ contains no trivial loops and parallel edges are not homotopic, F must be the outer face of G'G′. In particular, every face of G'G′ except the outer face has length at least 3. Consider the sum of the lengths of the faces of G'G′. Each edge contributes 2 to this sum and so the sum is equal to 2m'2m′. On the other hand, each face, except the outer face, contributes 3. Since n ⩾ 2n⩾2 and G'G′ is connected, the outer face contributes at least 1. In particular, the sum is at least 3f - 23f-2 where f is the number of faces of G'G′. Thus 3f - 2 ⩽ 2m'3f-2⩽2m′. By Euler’s formula, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 + m' - n \leqslant f \leqslant \frac{2}{3}(m' + 1)$$\end{document}2+m′-n⩽f⩽23(m′+1), and so m' ⩽ 3n - 4m′⩽3n-4, as desired. □ □

The bound in Proposition 2.4 is tight. For n = 2n=2, take the multigraph on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u, v\}$$\end{document}{u,v} consisting of edge uv, trivial loops at u and v as well as a loop at u that has all the other edges in its interior. For n ⩾ 3n⩾3, start with any simple plane triangulation on n vertices, with the outer face bounded by the triangle (u, v, w). Add a parallel edge uv so that w is no longer on the outer face boundary. Add a loop incident to u so that v is no longer on the outer face boundary. Finally, for each vertex x add a loop incident to x drawn in an internal face incident to x. The resulting drawing is non-homotopic, and with (3n - 6) + 1 + 1 + n = 4n - 4(3n-6)+1+1+n=4n-4 edges.

Suppose that two edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e = e_{s, a_1, \dotsc , a_{\ell - 1}, t}$$\end{document}e=es,a1,⋯,aℓ-1,t and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f = e_{s, b_1, \dotsc , b_{\ell ' - 1}, t}$$\end{document}f=es,b1,⋯,bℓ′-1,t between vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_s$$\end{document}vs and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_t$$\end{document}vt are homotopic. Then the signs of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_e$$\end{document}Pe and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_f$$\end{document}Pf must agree at each integer i strictly between s and t. Every crossing of one of the polynomials with the x-axis occurs at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x = m + \frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}x=m+12 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m \in \{s + 1, \dotsc , t - 1\}$$\end{document}m∈{s+1,⋯,t-1} and all such crossings except \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t - \frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}t-12 correspond to a change in sign of the polynomial. Hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_e$$\end{document}Pe and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_f$$\end{document}Pf must cross the x-axis at the same places (except possibly for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t - \frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}t-12). In particular, the sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{a_1, \dotsc , a_{\ell - 1}\}$$\end{document}{a1,⋯,aℓ-1} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{b_1, \dotsc , b_{\ell ' - 1}\}$$\end{document}{b1,⋯,bℓ′-1} are the same or differ by the element t - 1t-1. If they are the same, then e = fe=f. If they differ by the element t - 1t-1, then WLOG \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ' = \ell - 1$$\end{document}ℓ′=ℓ-1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_1 = b_1$$\end{document}a1=b1, ..., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{\ell - 2} = b_{\ell - 2}$$\end{document}aℓ-2=bℓ-2, and, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{\ell - 1} = t - 1$$\end{document}aℓ-1=t-1. But then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$xP_e(x) = (x - t + \frac{1}{2}) P_f(x)$$\end{document}xPe(x)=(x-t+12)Pf(x) and so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_e$$\end{document}Pe and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_f$$\end{document}Pf differ in sign at x = t - 1x=t-1 and so e and f are not homotopic. Therefore, the drawing of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{n, k}$$\end{document}Gn,k given is indeed non-homotopic.

Consider two distinct edges e and f of G. Both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_e$$\end{document}Pe and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_f$$\end{document}Pf are monic polynomials of degree k + 1k+1, so their difference is a polynomial of degree at most k, which has at most k roots. Thus the edges e and f cross at most k times. Now suppose that edges e and f are incident at vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_a$$\end{document}va. Then both e and f are monic polynomials of degree k + 1k+1 with a root at a, so their difference is a polynomial of degree at most k with a root at a. The root at a is not a crossing point of e and f, so e and f cross at most k - 1k-1 times. Similarly if e and f are parallel, then they cross at most k - 2k-2 times. In particular,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{n, k}$$\end{document}Gn,k has a monotone non-homotopic drawing in which each pair of edges cross at most k times,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \begin{document}$$G_{n, k + 1}$$\end{document}Gn,k+1 has a monotone non-homotopic drawing in which each pair of incident edges cross at most k times,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{n, k + 2}$$\end{document}Gn,k+2 has a monotone non-homotopic drawing in which each pair of parallel edges cross at most k times.Recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} Next we address the tightness of Theorem 1.3. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in \{2k, \dotsc , n\}$$\end{document}t∈{2k,⋯,n}.
